            - generic [ref=e64]:
              - heading "Advanced applications of synthetic biology technology in biosynthesis of bioactive compounds from medicinal plants" [level=1] [ref=e66]
              - generic [ref=e67]:
                - link "Yingjun Liu" [ref=e68] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Liu%20Y"[Author]

              - generic [ref=e86]:

                - link "41584698" [ref=e87] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41584698/
          - region "Article content" [ref=e88]:
            - generic [ref=e89]:
              - generic [ref=e90]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e91]: Abstract
                - paragraph [ref=e92]: Medicinal plants serve as valuable sources of bioactive compounds with critical applications across pharmaceutical, agricultural, and industrial sectors. Compared to chemical synthesis and plant extraction, synthetic biology offers a green, efficient, and sustainable alternative for producing bioactive compounds, which represents a state of art technology. However, this technology still faces several challenges, including overly long metabolic pathways, inadequate catalytic efficiency of key enzymes in the pathway, and incompatibility between gene elements and host cells, leading to low yields of target bioactive compounds. The development and application of regulatory tools in synthetic biology hold great promise for overcoming these obstacles. This review first summarizes the classification and biosynthesis of bioactive compounds based on structural types. Subsequently, recent advancements are outlined in regulation tools and their application in the heterologous production of bioactive compounds. This review aims to establish a foundation for the efficient production of bioactive compounds based on microbial cell factories. This not only has significant practical implications for reducing the resource consumption and environmental impact of traditional production methods, but also highlights the central role of synthetic biology in promoting the sustainable production of bioactive compounds derived from medicinal plants.
                - paragraph [ref=e94]:
                  - strong [ref=e95]: "Keywords:"
                  - text: bioactive compounds, medicinal plants, metabolic engineering, regulatory tools, synthetic biology
              - generic [ref=e96]:
                - heading "1. Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e97]: 1. Introduction
                - paragraph [ref=e98]:
                  - text: Medicinal plants produce diverse bioactive compounds with significant pharmacological properties (
                  - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e99] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#f0005"
                  - text: ) (
                  - link "Li et al., 2023" [ref=e100] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0485"
                  - text: ). Among these, flavonoids, terpenoids, and alkaloids constitute the three major groups of these compounds (
                  - link "Wang et al., 2019" [ref=e101] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0925"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Zhao et al., 2025" [ref=e102] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b1160"
                  - text: ). While non-essential for the plant’s survival, these compounds greatly benefit human health (
                  - link "Panchal and Tiwari, 2017" [ref=e103] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0690"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Adedeji and Babalola, 2020" [ref=e104] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0005"
                  - text: ), as demonstrated by examples like scutellarin’s neuroprotection (
                  - link "Sheng et al., 2023" [ref=e105] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0800"
                  - text: ), berberine’s anti-inflammatory effects (
                  - link "Wei et al., 2023" [ref=e106] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0955"
                  - text: ), and the neuroprotective potential of goji berry and ashwagandha extracts (
                  - link "Zhang & Wang, 2025" [ref=e107] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b1140"
                  - text: ). Widely used in pharmaceuticals, nutraceuticals, pesticides, cosmetics, and fine chemicals (
                  - link "Cravens et al., 2019" [ref=e108] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0150"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Taroncher et al., 2021" [ref=e109] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0855"
                  - text: ), they include clinically important drugs like artemisinin, morphine, paclitaxel, ginsenosides, lovastatin, and tanshinone (
                  - link "Buranasudja et al., 2021" [ref=e110] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0070"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Kowalczyk et al., 2024" [ref=e111] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0420"
                  - text: ), and icaritin, approved in China in 2022 for liver cancer (
                  - link "Wang, Wang, Liu, Yang, Zhang, & Kong, 2023" [ref=e112] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0935"
                  - text: ). The high demand is expected to grow, the flavonoid market alone is projected to rise from $149.77 million (2020) to $271.78 million by 2030 (
                  - link "Li et al., 2022" [ref=e113] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0475"
                  - text: ).
                - figure "Common activities of bioactive compounds in medicinal plants." [ref=e114]:
                  - heading "Fig. 1." [level=3] [ref=e115]
                  - paragraph [ref=e116]:
                    - link "Fig. 1 🔍" [ref=e117] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=12828132_gr1.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 1" [ref=e118]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e120] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/f0005/
                  - paragraph [ref=e122]: Common activities of bioactive compounds in medicinal plants.
                - paragraph [ref=e123]:
                  - text: Most bioactive compounds are obtained through plant extraction, including crocetin, glycyrrhizin, icaritin, salidroside, rosavin and so on (
                  - link "Qiu et al., 2022" [ref=e124] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0720"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Xia et al., 2022" [ref=e125] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0980"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Hong et al., 2023" [ref=e126] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0315"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Guo et al., 2024" [ref=e127] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0295"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Wu et al., 2025" [ref=e128] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0970"
                  - text: ). However, low concentrations in medicinal plants lead to high raw material consumption, and plant cultivation is time-consuming due to time, space, and climate, potentially threatening food security (
                  - link "Nomura et al., 2019" [ref=e129] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0655"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Wang et al., 2021" [ref=e130] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0920"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Shan et al., 2023" [ref=e131] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0780"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Zhou et al., 2024" [ref=e132] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b1205"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Li et al., 2024" [ref=e133] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0460"
                  - text: ). Chemical synthesis often suffers from long reaction pathways, complex reaction conditions, low product yields, high production costs, and generation of environmentally harmful by-products (
                  - link "Marchev et al., 2016" [ref=e134] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0590"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Nose et al., 2019" [ref=e135] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0660"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Wang et al., 2021" [ref=e136] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0920"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Xu et al., 2021" [ref=e137] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0995"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Li et al., 2024" [ref=e138] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0460"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Zhou et al., 2023" [ref=e139] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b1185"
                  - text: ). These limitations hinder stable supply of bioactive compounds, necessitating development of novel, cost-effective, resource-efficient, and safe production methods. Fortunately, synthetic biology has emerged as a highly promising production model due to its environmentally friendly, low-carbon, and sustainable attributes (
                  - link "Parkan et al., 2014" [ref=e140] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0700"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Yao et al., 2019" [ref=e141] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b1060"
                  - text: ).
                - paragraph [ref=e142]:
                  - text: Synthetic biology represents a novel and rapidly developing cross-disciplinary area within biotechnology (
                  - link "Glaser, 2016" [ref=e143] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0265"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Cravens et al., 2019" [ref=e144] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0150"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Jamieson et al., 2021" [ref=e145] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0350"
                  - text: ). By integrating systems biology, chemistry, computer science, and genetic engineering technology, it aims to design, simulate, and construct novel artificial biological systems or modify existing systems to perform innovative functions (
                  - link "Jiang et al., 2024" [ref=e146] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0380"
                  - text: ). Despite challenges from complex interactions requiring iterative optimization following the design-build-test-learn cycle, significant progress has been made in this field. For example, studies have demonstrated that engineered expression systems combined with metabolic engineering in yeast achieved 448.64 mg/L sanguinarine production (
                  - link "Gou et al., 2024" [ref=e147] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0280"
                  - text: ). Increased sanguinarine production, driven by its antimicrobial and antineoplastic activities, will expand its applications. In conclusion, synthetic biology offers a solution for producing bioactive compounds, addressing the supply–demand imbalance.
                - paragraph [ref=e148]:
                  - text: Advances in genomics and proteomics have clarified biosynthetic pathways of bioactive compounds in medicinal plants. A prime example is the detailed analysis of the paclitaxel biosynthetic pathway. Notably, paclitaxel, a pivotal anti-cancer drug, faced significant limitations due to the scarcity of plant resources and the complexity of its chemical synthesis. In this context, research successfully identified key missing enzymes enabling heterologous baccatin III production (
                  - link "Jiang et al., 2024" [ref=e149] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0380"
                  - text: ). Synthetic biology now enables the transfer of plant pathways to microbial hosts for efficient production of plant-derived drugs like artemisinic acid and vinblastine in engineered yeast (
                  - link "Gao et al., 2023" [ref=e150] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0230"
                  - text: ","
                  - link "Paddon et al., 2013" [ref=e151] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#b0685"
                  - text: ). This method not only addresses the challenge of obtaining natural products but also establishes heterologous biosynthesis as a critical research area in current scientific exploration.
                - paragraph [ref=e152]:
                  - text: However, synthetic biology faces several challenges in the biomanufacturing of bioactive compounds. The biosynthetic pathways of complex natural products, such as terpenoids, involve multi-enzyme cascades. The heterologous reconstruction of these pathways often results in suboptimal yields of the target bioactive compounds due to metabolic flux imbalance in host cells, insufficient activity of rate-limiting enzymes, limited cofactor supply, and incompatibility between genetic elements and host cells. Furthermore, engineered cell factories are prone to genetic instability and the accumulation of metabolic stress during large-scale fermentation processes, significantly compromising their robustness and production continuity. Regulatory tools in synthetic biology hold promise for overcoming these challenges. This section first categorizes bioactive compounds from medicinal plants according to structural diversity. Subsequently, recent advancements are outlined in regulatory tools and their applications in the heterologous production of bioactive compounds (
                  - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e153] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#f0010"
                  - text: ). The main goal of this paper is to lay a foundation for efficient, sustainable microbial production of bioactive compounds via synthetic biology technology.
                - figure "Schematic illustration of synthetic biology technology in the biosynthesis of bioactive compounds from medicinal plants." [ref=e154]:
                  - heading "Fig. 2." [level=3] [ref=e155]
                  - paragraph [ref=e156]:
                    - link "Fig. 2 🔍" [ref=e157] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=12828132_gr2.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 2" [ref=e158]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e160] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/f0010/
                  - paragraph [ref=e162]: Schematic illustration of synthetic biology technology in the biosynthesis of bioactive compounds from medicinal plants.
              - generic [ref=e163]:
                - heading "2. Overview of classification and biosynthesis of bioactive compounds from medicinal plants" [level=2] [ref=e164]: 2. Overview of classification and biosynthesis of bioactive compounds from medicinal plants
                - paragraph [ref=e165]:
                  - text: Bioactive compounds from medicinal plants constitute a diverse group of substances. Among these, flavonoids, terpenoids, and alkaloids stand out as the most extensively studied classes, exhibiting broad application potential. The study of the biosynthesis of these compounds has also received significant attention (
                  - link "Table 1" [ref=e166] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#t0005"
                  - text: ). In this section provide a comprehensive overview of the structure, distribution, function, and biosynthesis of these typical compounds.

                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e619] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: table/t0005/
                - generic [ref=e620]:
                  - heading "2.1. Flavonoids" [level=3] [ref=e621]
                  - generic [ref=e622]:
                    - heading "2.1.1. Structure, distribution, and function of flavonoids" [level=4] [ref=e623]
                    - paragraph [ref=e624]:
                      - text: Flavonoids are an important class of secondary metabolites in plants, and their structural feature is a diphenylpropane (C
                      - subscript [ref=e625]: "6"
                      - text: "-C"
                      - subscript [ref=e626]: "3"
                      - text: "-C"
                      - subscript [ref=e627]: "6"
                      - text: ) skeleton consisting of three rings (labeled A, B, and C in
                      - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e628] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0015"
                      - text: ) (
                      - link "Shen, Wang, Gan, Liu, Wang, & Jin, 2022" [ref=e629] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0790"
                      - text: ). Specifically, the differences in the levels of hydroxylation and oxidation of the C ring, together with changes in the substituents of the A and/or B rings, determine the unique structural classifications of flavonoids (
                      - link "Shamsudin et al., 2022" [ref=e630] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0775"
                      - text: ). These structural modifications lead to diverse subclasses, including flavones, flavanones, flavonols, flavanonols, flavan-3-ols, chalcones, isoflavones, and proanthocyanins (
                      - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e631] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0015"
                      - text: ). These compounds are crucial for plant defense mechanisms, as they help plants resist microbial infections and tolerate abiotic stresses (
                      - link "Zhuang et al., 2023" [ref=e632] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1220"
                      - text: ). Meanwhile, flavonoids can also offer various health benefits to humans because of their biological activities, including antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, anticancer, antimicrobial, antiviral, and anti-cardiovascular properties (Liu, Yang, Guo, Jiang, Zhu, & Yang, 2025). Moreover, flavonoids profoundly impact plant physiology by modulating processes like phototropism, pollen tube growth, and responses to environmental stressors, including UV radiation and microbial infections (
                      - link "Yang, Yang, Zhao, Liu, & Jiang, 2016" [ref=e633] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1035"
                      - text: ). Beyond their biological functions in plants, flavonoids are also widely present in tea, soybeans, citrus fruits, nuts, cereals, and berries, and serve as an indispensable component of the human diet (
                      - link "Zhong et al., 2023" [ref=e634] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1180"
                      - text: ). It is worth noting that research interest in flavonoids with antibacterial activity within the realm of food safety is progressively increasing. In conclusion, as research continues to advance, flavonoids will assume even greater significance and functionality.
                    - figure "Framework structures of the primary classes of flavonoids." [ref=e635]:
                      - heading "Fig. 3." [level=5] [ref=e636]
                      - paragraph [ref=e637]:
                        - link "Fig. 3 🔍" [ref=e638] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=12828132_gr3.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 3" [ref=e639]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e641] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/f0015/
                      - paragraph [ref=e643]: Framework structures of the primary classes of flavonoids.
                  - generic [ref=e644]:
                    - heading "2.1.2. Biosynthetic pathways of flavonoids" [level=4] [ref=e645]
                    - paragraph [ref=e646]:
                      - text: Currently, the biosynthetic pathways of flavonoids have been extensively studied and modified. This metabolic pathway can be categorized into four primary module (
                      - link "Fig. 4" [ref=e647] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0020"
                      - text: "): Aromatic amino acid (precursor 1) biosynthesis module: This section focuses on the production of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e648]: L
                      - text: "-tyrosine and"
                      - emphasis [ref=e649]: L
                      - text: "-phenylalanine, which act as precursors for flavonoid formation. These amino acids are synthesized via the native shikimate pathway, where phosphoenolpyruvate (PEP) and erythrose-4-phosphate (E4P) are converted into prephenate (PPA), eventually leading to the generation of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e650]: L
                      - text: "-tyrosine or"
                      - emphasis [ref=e651]: L
                      - text: "-phenylalanine. Malonyl-CoA (precursor 2) biosynthesis module: Malonyl-CoA, a key biosynthetic precursor for flavonoid synthesis, is generated from acetyl-CoA through the catalytic action of acetyl-CoA carboxylase. Acetyl-CoA itself originates from multiple pathways, such as the citrate cycle, pyruvate dehydrogenase (PDH) complex, pyruvate bypass, and"
                      - emphasis [ref=e652]: β
                      - text: "-oxidation processes. (2"
                      - emphasis [ref=e653]: S
                      - text: ")-Naringenin biosynthesis module: One molecule of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e654]: p
                      - text: "-coumaroyl-CoA (derived from aromatic amino acids) and three molecules of malonyl-CoA undergo Claisen cyclization and dehydration reactions mediated by chalcone synthase (CHS) and chalcone isomerase (CHI), resulting in the formation of naringenin. It is worth noting that it is an intermediate for the synthesis of different subclasses of various flavonoids. Naringenin derived module: Following the formation of the flavanone backbone, the biosynthetic pathway branches out into multiple pathways, each leading to the production of distinct flavonoid subclasses. Isoflavones are synthesized via isoflavone synthase (IFS), while flavones are produced by flavone synthase (FNS). Flavanols are generated from the oxidation of flavanones catalyzed by flavanone 3-dioxygenase (F3H). Additional structural modifications, mediated by enzymes such as flavonol synthase (FLS), dihydroflavonol reductase (DFR), and anthocyanidin synthase (ANS), along with methyltransferases and prenyltransferases, result in the formation of a diverse array of structurally complex flavonoids, including flavonols, anthocyanins, and proanthocyanidins ("
                      - link "Chen et al., 2019" [ref=e655] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0125"
                      - text: ).
                    - figure "Biosynthetic pathway of flavonoids. TCA-tricarboxylic acid." [ref=e656]:
                      - heading "Fig. 4." [level=5] [ref=e657]
                      - paragraph [ref=e658]:
                        - link "Fig. 4 🔍" [ref=e659] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=12828132_gr4.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 4" [ref=e660]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e662] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/f0020/
                      - paragraph [ref=e664]: Biosynthetic pathway of flavonoids. TCA-tricarboxylic acid.
                    - paragraph [ref=e665]: In conclusion, through the examination and modification of each of these components, it is possible to customize the flavonoid biosynthesis pathway. This customization can promote the production of a wide variety of flavonoid compounds that possess potential significance in industrial applications.
                  - generic [ref=e666]:
                    - heading "2.1.3. De novo synthesis of flavonoids using cell factories" [level=4] [ref=e667]
                    - paragraph [ref=e668]:
                      - text: Recent advances in synthetic biology have facilitated the production of flavonoids through microbial cell factories, providing a sustainable and scalable alternative compared to traditional plant extraction. For example, icaritin, the primary active constituent of the traditional Chinese herb
                      - emphasis [ref=e669]: Epimedii Folium
                      - text: and a critical component in the liver cancer drug. Acolaritin, has been successfully synthesized from glucose in
                      - emphasis [ref=e670]: E. coli
                      - text: (19.7 mg/L) (
                      - link "Wang et al., 2021" [ref=e671] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0920"
                      - text: ). In parallel, icariin, another anticancer flavonoid, was synthesized in
                      - emphasis [ref=e672]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: (130 μg/L) (
                      - link "An et al., 2023" [ref=e673] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0020"
                      - text: ). These breakthroughs highlight the potential of synthetic biology to produce complex compounds originating from plants, which hold considerable therapeutic value.
                    - paragraph [ref=e674]:
                      - text: Besides these bioactive flavonoids, the production of critical intermediates like (2
                      - emphasis [ref=e675]: S
                      - text: )-naringenin has garnered significant attention. A study reported a yield of 588 mg/L in a 5-L fermentation system using
                      - emphasis [ref=e676]: E. coli
                      - text: (
                      - link "Zhou, Hao, & Zhou, 2020" [ref=e677] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1195"
                      - text: ). Similarly, another study obtained a yield of 1.1 g/L by employing
                      - emphasis [ref=e678]: S. cerevisia
                      - text: (
                      - link "Zhang, Yu, Lyu, Zeng, & Zhou, 2021" [ref=e679] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1115"
                      - text: ). Moreover,
                      - emphasis [ref=e680]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: has also been engineered to produce 12.4 g/L of (2
                      - emphasis [ref=e681]: S
                      - text: )-naringenin in a 1-L fermentation system (
                      - link "Sáez-Sáe, Wang, Marella, Sudarsan, Cernuda Pastor, & Borodina, 2020" [ref=e682] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0760"
                      - text: ). Beyond canonical flavonoids, other compounds have also been successfully synthesized through whole-cell catalysis. Research demonstrated the synthesis of baicalein from
                      - emphasis [ref=e683]: L
                      - text: "-phenylalanine as the substrate using"
                      - emphasis [ref=e684]: E. coli
                      - text: ", achieving a yield of 271.6 mg/L ("
                      - link "Ji, Li, Xu, Ren, & Wang, 2021" [ref=e685] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0370"
                      - text: ). Similarly, studies synthesized kaempferol from
                      - emphasis [ref=e686]: p
                      - text: "-coumaric acid as the precursor in a 1.5-L bioreactor using"
                      - emphasis [ref=e687]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: (66.3 mg/L) (
                      - link "Duan et al., 2017" [ref=e688] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0200"
                      - text: ). Further expanding host diversity, research optimized taxifolin biosynthesis from glucose by reprogramming
                      - emphasis [ref=e689]: Y. lipolytica
                      - text: ", yielding 134 mg/L in a 3-L fermentation system ("
                      - link "Lv, Edwards, Zhou, & Xu, 2019" [ref=e690] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0570"
                      - text: ). However, due to excessively long biosynthetic pathways and insufficient catalytic efficiency of key enzymes, the production titers of these compounds remain suboptimal, necessitating further optimization (
                      - link "Table 1" [ref=e691] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#t0005"
                      - text: ). All in all, these findings highlight the versatility and efficiency of microbial cell factories in producing structurally diverse flavonoids.
                - generic [ref=e692]:
                  - heading "2.2. Terpenoids" [level=3] [ref=e693]
                  - generic [ref=e694]:
                    - heading "2.2.1. Structure, distribution, and function of terpenoids" [level=4] [ref=e695]
                    - paragraph [ref=e696]:
                      - text: Terpenoids, also known as isoprenoids, constitute one of the most structurally diverse classes of natural products, with over 80 000 identified compounds to date. These compounds are defined by their molecular formula (C
                      - subscript [ref=e697]: "5"
                      - text: H
                      - subscript [ref=e698]: "8"
                      - text: )
                      - emphasis [ref=e699]:
                        - subscript [ref=e700]: "n"
                      - text: ", where ‘"
                      - emphasis [ref=e701]: "n"
                      - text: "’ represents the number of isoprene units. Based on the number of isoprene units and their structural organization, terpenoids are classified into several major groups: Hemiterpenes (C"
                      - subscript [ref=e702]: "5"
                      - text: ), monoterpenes (C
                      - subscript [ref=e703]: "10"
                      - text: ), sesquiterpenes (C
                      - subscript [ref=e704]: "15"
                      - text: ), diterpenes (C
                      - subscript [ref=e705]: "20"
                      - text: ), sesterterpenes (C
                      - subscript [ref=e706]: "25"
                      - text: ), triterpenes (C
                      - subscript [ref=e707]: "30"
                      - text: ), tetraterpenes (C
                      - subscript [ref=e708]: "40"
                      - text: ), and polyterpenes (C
                      - subscript [ref=e709]: ">40"
                      - text: ) (
                      - link "Câmara et al., 2024" [ref=e710] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0080"
                      - text: ). Representative examples of terpenoids include geraniol and menthol (monoterpenes), artemisinin and farnesol (sesquiterpenes), paclitaxel and triptolide (diterpenes), ursolic acid and oleanolic acid (triterpenes), and lutein and carotene (tetraterpenes) (
                      - link "Bergman & Dudareva, 2024" [ref=e711] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0035"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e712]:
                      - text: Terpenoids are believed to have originated early in cellular evolution, playing a crucial role in the formation of primitive membranes with early precursors, such as diphytane glycerol ethers and polyprenols (
                      - link "Zwenger & Basu, 2008" [ref=e713] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1230"
                      - text: ). They have since evolved into a highly diverse group found across a wide range of organisms, particularly in plants. Terpenoids are vital for plant growth, defense against pathogens and herbivores, resistance to stresses, and interactions with other organisms (
                      - link "Cheng, Lou, Mao, Lu, Wang, & Chen, 2007" [ref=e714] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0140"
                      - text: ;
                      - link "Yang et al., 2020" [ref=e715] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1050"
                      - text: ). Many terpenoids exhibit potent toxicity, serving as chemical defenses and contributing significantly to their medicinal and economic value. They find applications in industries such as fragrances, pharmaceuticals, biofuels, and natural rubber (
                      - link "Bergman, Davis, & Phillips, 2019" [ref=e716] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0040"
                      - text: ). For instance, monoterpenes with anticancer activity, including perilla alcohol and geraniol, sesquiterpenes like artemisinin and costunolide, diterpenes like paclitaxel, and triterpenes like betulinic acid and cucurbitacins have been developed into drugs. The volatile monoterpene menthol is commonly used in fragrances, cosmetics, and flavorings, while pyrethroids and limonoid compounds serve as insecticides, and bisabolene, limonene, and pinene are used in biofuels (
                      - link "Huang et al., 2023" [ref=e717] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0325"
                      - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e718]:
                    - heading "2.2.2. Biosynthetic pathways of terpenoids" [level=4] [ref=e719]
                    - paragraph [ref=e720]:
                      - text: "The biosynthetic pathway of terpenoids can be broadly categorized into three main segments: The upstream metabolic pathway, which primarily involves the conversion of carbon sources (e.g., glucose and xylose) into precursor molecules for terpene synthesis, including pyruvic acid, glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate, and acetyl-CoA, along with cofactors and ATP via central carbon metabolism, the midstream metabolic pathway, specifically the synthesis pathways of the C"
                      - subscript [ref=e721]: "5"
                      - text: skeleton isoprene diphosphate (IPP) and dimethylallyl diphosphate (DMAPP) (
                      - link "Kuzuyama & Seto, 2012" [ref=e722] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0435"
                      - text: ), and the downstream terpene synthesis pathway, which involves the assembly of terpene skeletons and their subsequent modifications by terpene synthases, leading to a diverse array of functional terpene compounds (
                      - link "Fig. 5" [ref=e723] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0025"
                      - text: ) (
                      - link "Volke, Rohwer, Fischer, & Jennewein, 2019" [ref=e724] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0910"
                      - text: "). The midstream metabolic pathway is uniquely bifurcated into two distinct routes: The 2-"
                      - emphasis [ref=e725]: C
                      - text: "-methyl-"
                      - emphasis [ref=e726]: D
                      - text: "-erythritol-4-phosphate (MEP) pathway and the mevalonate (MVA) pathway, each distinguished by their specific sources of IPP and DMAPP. Notably, the MEP pathway utilizes pyruvate and glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate as precursors and is predominantly located in plant plastids, cyanobacteria, and most bacteria. Conversely, the MVA pathway utilizes acetyl-CoA as a precursor and is chiefly found in archaea, specific bacteria, plant cytoplasm, and the majority of eukaryotes ("
                      - link "Di et al., 2023" [ref=e727] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0190"
                      - text: ). Notably, some archaea possess an alternative form of the MVA pathway that can bypasses mevalonate pyrophosphate to generate IPP. To address the challenges posed by the long natural terpenoid synthesis pathways (MEP and MVA) and the complexities of metabolic regulation, scientists have engineered artificial terpenoid synthesis pathways. An example of such a pathway is the isopreneol utilization pathway (IUP), which synthesizes IPP in just two steps using isopreneol as a substrate (
                      - link "Zhang et al., 2023" [ref=e728] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1150"
                      - text: ).
                    - figure "Biosynthetic pathway of terpenoids. FPP-farnesyl diphosphate, GGPP-geranylgeranyl diphosphate, GFPP-geranylfarnesyl diphosphate." [ref=e729]:
                      - heading "Fig. 5." [level=5] [ref=e730]
                      - paragraph [ref=e731]:
                        - link "Fig. 5 🔍" [ref=e732] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=12828132_gr5.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 5" [ref=e733]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e735] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/f0025/
                      - paragraph [ref=e737]: Biosynthetic pathway of terpenoids. FPP-farnesyl diphosphate, GGPP-geranylgeranyl diphosphate, GFPP-geranylfarnesyl diphosphate.
                  - generic [ref=e738]:
                    - heading "2.2.3. De novo synthesis of terpenoids using cell factories" [level=4] [ref=e739]
                    - paragraph [ref=e740]:
                      - text: Numerous studies have demonstrated the successful biosynthesis of terpenoid compounds in microbial cell factories, highlighting the potential of these systems for producing high-value bioactive molecules. For example, quillaja saponin-21 (QS-21), a highly effective vaccine adjuvant approved for use in multiple vaccines, was synthesized by the Jay Keasling research group. The team fine-tuned the metabolic flux and optimized the expression and balance of 38 heterologous enzymes, ultimately producing QS-21 analogs through a biosynthetic platform (
                      - link "Liu et al., 2024" [ref=e741] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0560"
                      - text: ). Additionally, the same research group engineered
                      - emphasis [ref=e742]: S. cerevisia
                      - text: to produce artemisinin, the precursor to the anti-malarial drug artemisinin. This breakthrough not only stabilized the global supply of artemisinin, but also made it more accessible and affordable, particularly in malaria-endemic regions (
                      - link "Paddon et al., 2013" [ref=e743] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0685"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e744]:
                      - text: Beyond terpenoids with significant medicinal value, various terpenoids with versatile funticons are also being widely synthesized. For instance, research investigations examined 122 sesquiterpene scaffolds, identifying nine high-yielding strains and assessing their potential as high-energy aviation fuels (
                      - link "Huang et al., 2023" [ref=e745] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0325"
                      - text: ). Similarly, studies achieved concentrations of 966.55 mg/L nerol and 87.20 mg/L borneol in
                      - emphasis [ref=e746]: E. coli
                      - text: (
                      - link "Lei, Qiu, Wu, Qiao, Qiao, & Zhao, 2021" [ref=e747] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0450"
                      - text: ). Additionally, research reported the successful synthesis of glycyrrhizin (GL) and glycyrrhetic acid 3-
                      - emphasis [ref=e748]: O
                      - text: "-mono-"
                      - emphasis [ref=e749]: β
                      - text: "-"
                      - emphasis [ref=e750]: D
                      - text: "-glucuronide (GAMG) in"
                      - emphasis [ref=e751]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: ", yielding production levels of 5.98 mg/L and 2.31 mg/L for GL and GAMG, respectively ("
                      - link "Xu et al., 2021" [ref=e752] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0995"
                      - text: ). These studies underscore the growing potential of microbial cell factories to efficiently produce terpenoid compounds for diverse applications, including drug development, industrial materials, and sustainable energy solutions. However, their output remains relatively low and requires further enhancement (
                      - link "Table 1" [ref=e753] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#t0005"
                      - text: ).
                - generic [ref=e754]:
                  - heading "2.3. Alkaloids" [level=3] [ref=e755]
                  - generic [ref=e756]:
                    - heading "2.3.1. Structure, distribution, and function of alkaloids" [level=4] [ref=e757]
                    - paragraph [ref=e758]:
                      - text: Alkaloids, a class of nitrogen-containing organic compounds that occur naturally and are typically derived from amino acids, exhibit complex and diverse molecular structures. These structures primarily consist of carbon, hydrogen, and nitrogen, with the inclusion of oxygen, sulfur, chlorine, bromine, and phosphorus (
                      - link "Bui, Rodríguez-López, & Dang, 2023" [ref=e759] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0065"
                      - text: ). The structural characteristics of alkaloids, including cyclic structures and a variety of functional groups, significantly influence their biological activity and pharmacological effects. Alkaloids can be categorized based on their chemical structure and nitrogen source into several types, such as indole (e.g., colchicine), pyridine (e.g., nicotine), tropane (e.g., atropine), and isoquinoline (e.g., morphine) (
                      - link "Schläger & Dräger, 2016" [ref=e760] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0765"
                      - text: ).
                    - paragraph [ref=e761]:
                      - text: Alkaloids are widely distributed in plants, animals, bacteria, and fungi, particularly as secondary metabolites in plants. They play critical roles in plant defense mechanisms against biotic stressors (e.g., pathogens and herbivores) and in attracting pollinators to promote reproduction (
                      - link "Jha & Mohamed, 2022" [ref=e762] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0360"
                      - text: ). Currently, approximately 10 000 alkaloids have been identified, with an estimated 100 new compounds discovered annually. Therein, terpenoid indole alkaloids (TIAs) constitute the largest class and are primarily found in plant families like apocynaceae, magnoliaceae, and rubiaceae (
                      - link "Taha, 2003" [ref=e763] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0840"
                      - text: ). For humans, alkaloids exhibit significant pharmacological activities and are widely utilized in drug development (
                      - link "Mishra, Tripathi, Kishore, Singh, Singh, & Tiwari, 2017" [ref=e764] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0620"
                      - text: ). They have diverse therapeutic effects, including anticancer, antibacterial, analgesic, and antidepressant properties (
                      - link "Ng, Or, & Ip, 2015" [ref=e765] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0640"
                      - text: ). Notable examples include morphine and codeine, which are used for pain relief and anesthesia, and nicotine, which has stimulating effects in tobacco products. The remarkable diversity and potent biological activities of alkaloids make them indispensable components of medicinal plants, offering abundant resources and broad application prospects for the development of drugs and natural products (
                      - link "Debnath et al., 2018" [ref=e766] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0175"
                      - text: ).
                  - generic [ref=e767]:
                    - heading "2.3.2. Biosynthetic pathways of alkaloids" [level=4] [ref=e768]
                    - paragraph [ref=e769]:
                      - text: Alkaloids represent a structurally diverse group of compounds whose biosynthetic pathways exhibit shared characteristics. These pathways generally initiate with amino acid precursors and most alkaloids are derived from amino acids such as ornithine, lysine, phenylalanine, tyrosine, tryptophan, and histidine. The initial step usually entails the decarboxylation of these amino acids, a process catalyzed by decarboxylases and facilitated by the cofactor pyridoxal phosphate (PLP). In many alkaloid biosynthesis pathways, the amino group of the amino acid reacts with the aldehyde group of PLP to form an imine intermediate. This intermediate subsequently undergoes decarboxylation, generating a carbanion that is protonated and hydrolyzed to produce the corresponding amine, thereby regenerating PLP for subsequent reactions (
                      - link "Fig. 6" [ref=e770] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0030"
                      - text: ) (
                      - link "Facchini & De Luca, 2008" [ref=e771] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0210"
                      - text: ).
                    - figure "Biosynthetic pathway of alkaloids." [ref=e772]:
                      - heading "Fig. 6." [level=5] [ref=e773]
                      - paragraph [ref=e774]:
                        - link "Fig. 6 🔍" [ref=e775] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=12828132_gr6.jpg
                          - img "Fig. 6" [ref=e776]
                          - text: 🔍
                      - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e778] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: figure/f0030/
                      - paragraph [ref=e780]: Biosynthetic pathway of alkaloids.
                    - paragraph [ref=e781]:
                      - text: Monoterpenoid indole alkaloids, such as vinblastine, are bioactive compounds from plants with significant biological activity. (Yu & Yong, 2021). Herein, this study takes it as a representative example for introduction Strictosidine, a key skeletal compound in the biosynthesis of monoterpene indole alkaloids, is produced by the fusion of tryptamine and secologanin (
                      - link "Zhu et al., 2015" [ref=e782] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1210"
                      - text: "). This process, known as the upstream synthesis pathway, primarily occurs via two pathways: The iridoid pathway and the indole pathway. Under the action of strictosidine synthase (STR), strictosidine is formed by the condensation of tryptamine and secologanin. The downstream synthesis pathway involves the generation of various monoterpenoid indole alkaloids, starting from strictosidine as the initial precursor and facilitated by diverse enzymes ("
                      - link "Yamazaki et al., 2003" [ref=e783] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1020"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "Vadhel et al., 2023" [ref=e784] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0900"
                      - text: ). Well-studied biosynthetic pathways involve those of vinblastine, tabersonine, vincristine, heteroyohimbine and their derivatives from
                      - emphasis [ref=e785]: Catharanthus roseus
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e786]: Vinca minor
                      - text: L., as well as ajmaline from
                      - emphasis [ref=e787]: Rauvolfia verticillata
                      - text: (Lour.) Baill. (Yu & Yong, 2021).
                  - generic [ref=e788]:
                    - heading "2.3.3. De novo synthesis of alkaloids using cell factories" [level=4] [ref=e789]
                    - paragraph [ref=e790]:
                      - text: Advancements in synthetic biology have enabled the
                      - emphasis [ref=e791]: de novo
                      - text: synthesis of alkaloids through engineered microorganisms. A landmark achievement was made by Christina D. Smolke’s research group, which successfully achieved the first artificial biosynthesis of tropane alkaloids in
                      - emphasis [ref=e792]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: . They designed a biosynthetic pathway involving five functional modules to produce compounds like hyoscyamine and scopolamine from simple sugars and amino acids. This process involved identifying missing enzymes, engineering proteins for functional expression, and optimizing fermentation conditions, ultimately resulting in a yield of 60 mg/L (
                      - link "Srinivasan & Smolke, 2020" [ref=e793] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0820"
                      - text: ). Similarly, research by the Keasling group engineered
                      - emphasis [ref=e794]: S. cerevisiae
                      - text: to produce monoterpenoid indole alkaloids (MIAs) and their halogenated derivatives. They also made significant progress in producing vinblastine precursors and other complex drugs by introducing and fine-tuning numerous genes in yeast (
                      - link "Bradley et al., 2023" [ref=e795] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0055"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "Zhang et al., 2022" [ref=e796] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b1100"
                      - text: ). To address temporal regulation challenges, Research developed a temperature-responsive gene expression system based on intein-mediated control (SIMTeGES), enabling the complete biosynthesis of sanguinarine and its halogenated derivatives in yeast (
                      - link "Gou et al., 2024" [ref=e797] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0280"
                      - text: ). Concurrently, Studies expanded the alkaloid biosynthetic toolkit by characterizing NascB, a promiscuous P450 enzyme enabling efficient pyrroloindoline scaffold formation (
                      - link "Tian et al., 2018" [ref=e798] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#b0885"
                      - text: ). Nevertheless, because of the overly extended biosynthetic pathways and the inadequate catalytic performance of critical enzymes, the yield of alkaloids is still less than ideal, thus requiring additional improvements (
                      - link "Table 1" [ref=e799] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#t0005"
                      - text: ). Collectively, these studies contribute to the establishment of microbial platforms for sustainable production of essential alkaloid compounds from medicinal plants.
                - generic [ref=e800]:
                  - heading "2.4. Other compounds" [level=3] [ref=e801]
                  - paragraph [ref=e802]:
                    - text: In addition to flavonoids, terpenes, and alkaloids, synthetic biology and metabolic engineering have enabled the biosynthesis of diverse bioactive compounds, including glycosides (
                    - link "Guan et al., 2025" [ref=e803] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0290"
                    - text: ), polyketides (
                    - link "Gayen et al., 2020" [ref=e804] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0245"
                    - text: ), non-ribosomal peptides (
                    - link "Chen et al., 2023" [ref=e805] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0120"
                    - text: ), saccharides (
                    - link "Rana & Upadhyay, 2020" [ref=e806] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0730"
                    - text: ), ribosomally synthesized peptides (Liu, Yang, Guo, Jiang, & Sun, 2024), and post-translationally modified peptides (
                    - link "Wang et al., 2022" [ref=e807] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0930"
                    - text: ). These compounds hold significant potential for applications in medicine, agriculture, and food science. Recent advances in microbial biosynthesis have achieved impressive yields through innovative strategies, such as the selection of optimal microbial chassis (e.g.,
                    - emphasis [ref=e808]: E. coli
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e809]: S. cerevisiae
                    - text: ", and"
                    - emphasis [ref=e810]: Y. lipolytica
                    - text: ) and the optimization of pathways, synthetic promoters, and enzyme co-expression. Consequently, these developments have led to more robust microbial strains and cost-effective fermentation processes, enabling large-scale production of medicinal plant-derived compounds. In the following sections, we will explore the cutting-edge tools and strategies driving this field, which are shaping the future of biotechnological innovation.
              - generic [ref=e811]:
                - heading "3. Development of synthetic biology regulatory tools and their applications" [level=2] [ref=e812]: 3. Development of synthetic biology regulatory tools and their applications
                - paragraph [ref=e813]:
                  - text: Microbial cell factories possess the remarkable ability to convert inexpensive biomass feedstocks into high-value compounds, serving as a cornerstone platform for biomanufacturing. However, several challenges hinder the construction of efficient microbial cell factories using synthetic biology, including overly long metabolic pathways, insufficient catalytic efficiency of key enzymes in the pathway, and incompatibility between genetic elements and host cells, all of which result in low yields of target bioactive compounds. The development and application of synthetic biology regulatory tools offer significant potential by enabling precise control over microbial metabolism. In this section introduces some regulatory tools in synthetic biology that enhance the biosynthesis of bioactive compounds (
                  - link "Fig. 7" [ref=e814] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: "#f0035"
                  - text: ). First, foundational tools such as gene expression regulatory parts and DNA assembly technique enable modular construction of gene expression cassette. Second, genome editing technologies and biosensor-based dynamic regulation systems allow real-time pathway optimization through host genome modification and feedback-controlled expression. Third, emerging strategies including genetic code expansion and modular co-culture systems address challenges in pathway compartmentalization and metabolic burden distribution. Collectively, these multi-level interventions demonstrate how synthetic biology tools overcome intrinsic limitations of microbial cell factories, highlighting their critical role in synthesizing pharmaceutically bioactive compounds and advancing biomanufacturing applications.
                - figure [ref=e815]:
                  - heading "Fig. 7." [level=3] [ref=e816]
                  - paragraph [ref=e817]:
                    - link "Fig. 7 🔍" [ref=e818] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&p=PMC3&id=12828132_gr7.jpg
                      - img "Fig. 7" [ref=e819]
                      - text: 🔍
                  - link "Open in a new tab" [ref=e821] [cursor=pointer]:
                    - /url: figure/f0035/
                  - paragraph [ref=e823]:
                    - text: Synthetic biology regulatory tools. (A) Gene expression regulatory parts, including promoter, ribosome binding sites (RBS), and terminator. (B) The illustrative figure for BroBrick technology and Gibson assembly. (C) Scheme of the clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeats (CRISPR)/CRISPR-associated protein 9 (Cas9) system and the causes recombination (Cre)/locus of X-over P1 (loxP) system for genome editing. (D) The
                    - emphasis [ref=e824]: "N"
                    - text: "-acyl homoserine lactone synthase (LuxI)/transcriptional regulator (LuxR) quorum sensing system in"
                    - emphasis [ref=e825]: Vibrio fischeri
                    - text: . LuxI is the signal molecule AHL synthetase. LuxI serves as the enzyme responsible for synthesizing AHL signal molecules. When LuxR binds to AHL, it triggers the activation of gene transcription downstream of the P
                    - subscript [ref=e826]: LuxI
                    - text: promoter. (E) The schematic diagram for genetic code expansion technology. (F) Collaborative interactions within co-culture systems enhance the yield of the desired product.
                - generic [ref=e827]:
                  - heading "3.1. Gene expression regulatory parts" [level=3] [ref=e828]
                  - paragraph [ref=e829]:
                    - text: Achieving precise control over gene expression stands as one of the primary challenges in synthetic biology, particularly when aiming to optimize metabolic pathways (
                    - link "Yang et al., 2024" [ref=e830] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b1040"
                    - text: ). To tackle this issue, it is crucial to conduct a systematic evaluation of the functional attributes of gene expression components. For example, promoters modulate the level of gene expression by governing the rate of transcription initiation, ribosomal binding sites (RBS) affect translational efficiency, and terminators ensure the precision of transcriptional termination (
                    - link "De Nijs et al., 2020" [ref=e831] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0170"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Ito et al., 2020" [ref=e832] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0340"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Zhang et al., 2022" [ref=e833] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b1100"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Xiao et al., 2024" [ref=e834] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0985"
                    - text: ). Notably, for model microorganisms such as
                    - emphasis [ref=e835]: E. coli
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e836]: Bacillus subtilis
                    - text: ", and"
                    - emphasis [ref=e837]: S. cerevisiae
                    - text: ", scientists have established multi-dimensional databases. These databases quantify the expression strength and dynamic range of various elements, offering modular resources for metabolic engineering applications ("
                    - link "Fig. 7A" [ref=e838] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#f0035"
                    - text: ). Building on this framework, the design of metabolic pathways frequently entails integrating multiple gene expression modules. For instance, coupling promoters with RBS can harmonize the expression levels of enzymes that limit reaction rates, while weaker terminators can reduce the impact of transcriptional read-through on host metabolism (
                    - link "Chen et al., 2019" [ref=e839] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0125"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Lee et al., 2024" [ref=e840] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0445"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Liu et al., 2022" [ref=e841] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0520"
                    - text: ). Collectively, these gene expression elements serve as the foundation for developing highly efficient microbial cell factories.
                  - paragraph [ref=e842]:
                    - text: The control of transcription is the primary regulatory step in bacterial gene expression (
                    - link "Jensen & Galburt, 2021" [ref=e843] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0355"
                    - text: ). Therein, the promoter serves as a critical transcriptional signal that precisely regulates target gene expression at the transcriptional level by modulating RNA polymerase recruitment efficiency (
                    - link "Brázda et al., 2021" [ref=e844] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0060"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Cazier and Blazeck, 2021" [ref=e845] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0090"
                    - text: ). Leveraging this characteristic, promoter engineering has been widely applied to optimize biosynthetic pathways. For example, studies demonstrated that eriocitrin production was enhanced eriocitrin production 5-fold by pairing promoters of varying intensities to balance metabolic fluxes between glycosylation reactions and glycosylated donor synthesis (
                    - link "Xiao et al., 2023" [ref=e846] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0990"
                    - text: ). Meanwhile, research expanded the promoter toolkit for
                    - emphasis [ref=e847]: O. polymorpha
                    - text: by integrating transcriptomic data, constructing a library comprising 13 constitutive, two growth-stage-dependent, and two heterozygous promoters. Using this strategy, the engineered strain YY150U achieved a
                    - emphasis [ref=e848]: β
                    - text: "-elemene yield of 5.24 g/L during shake flask-fed batch fermentation ("
                    - link "Ye et al., 2024" [ref=e849] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b1075"
                    - text: ). In addition, by manipulating the inducible promoter to alleviate product toxicity and optimize the expression level of geraniol synthase, the production of geraniol was successfully increased to 1.68 mg/L (
                    - link "Jiang et al., 2017" [ref=e850] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0385"
                    - text: ). These examples illustrate how promoter engineering can enhance target product synthesis through mechanisms such as coordinating carbon flux distribution and regulating rate-limiting enzyme catalytic efficiency. However, the existing studies continue to encounter challenges, including inadequate characterization of endogenous promoters, a limited dynamic range for regulation, and insufficient host adaptability. In particular, the trade-off between component orthogonality and the global allocation of metabolic fluxes within synthetic pathways remains unresolved. Consequently, the development of synthetic promoters with an extended dynamic range and enhanced host compatibility will represent a critical direction for advancing the industrial application of this technology.
                  - paragraph [ref=e851]:
                    - text: Terminators are also one of the crucial regulatory elements in gene expression (
                    - link "Ray-Soni et al., 2016" [ref=e852] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0745"
                    - text: ). Terminators can influence mRNA stability and abundance, rendering them essential for the completion of the transcription process (
                    - link "Curran et al., 2013" [ref=e853] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0160"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Yamanishi et al., 2013" [ref=e854] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b1015"
                    - text: ). Given these characteristics, terminators have been extensively utilized in metabolic engineering and synthetic biology. For instance, research achieved a 35% and 34% increase in the production of
                    - emphasis [ref=e855]: α
                    - text: "-amylase and poly-"
                    - emphasis [ref=e856]: γ
                    - text: "-glutamic acid by employing an artificially designed short terminator T1 ("
                    - link "Rao et al., 2022" [ref=e857] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0740"
                    - text: ). To identify termination sequences suitable for regulating protein expression levels in
                    - emphasis [ref=e858]: P. pastoris
                    - text: ", research developed a ‘terminator catalog’ through the evaluation of 72 sequences that exhibited a regulatory range of 17-fold ("
                    - link "Ito et al., 2020" [ref=e859] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0340"
                    - text: ). Furthermore, research into synthetic terminators have also been conducted. Study designed and constructed a series of components ranging from 38 to 75 bp as minimal terminators, with the most effective variants enhancing fluorescence intensity by up to 3.7 times (
                    - link "Curran et al., 2015" [ref=e860] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0165"
                    - text: ). Despite current limitations in this field, its potential within synthetic biology has garnered considerable interest from researchers. Future studies are expected to further elucidate the role of terminators in regulating gene expression within microbial cells.
                  - paragraph [ref=e861]:
                    - text: In addition to promoters and terminators, ribosome binding sites (RBS) play a crucial role in translation level regulation (
                    - link "Rao et al., 2024" [ref=e862] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0735"
                    - text: ). They directly influence both the abundance and quality of proteins by affecting translation efficiency and fidelity (
                    - link "Faure et al., 2016" [ref=e863] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0215"
                    - text: ). Consequently, RBS libraries with varying strengths serve as valuable tools for gene expression regulation, thus alleviating the problem of insufficient catalytic efficiency of key enzymes (
                    - link "Mutalik et al., 2013" [ref=e864] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0635"
                    - text: ). For instance, studies optimized the ribosome binding site of sesquiterpene synthase, resulting in the optimal strain R10 producing 151.25 mg/L of
                    - emphasis [ref=e865]: β
                    - text: "-elemene ("
                    - link "Fordjour et al., 2023" [ref=e866] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0220"
                    - text: ). Similarly, research engineered the strain CgSq27p to produce 15.15 mg/L
                    - emphasis [ref=e867]: α
                    - text: "-amyrin by modulating the expression level of the key enzyme MdaASCgco using RBS sequences with varying strengths ("
                    - link "Li et al., 2025" [ref=e868] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0465"
                    - text: ). These studies not only highlight the significant potential of RBS optimization in metabolic engineering but also offer valuable insights for analogous research endeavors. Overall, as a central component of translational regulation, RBS plays a crucial role in optimizing gene expression. By precisely tuning the RBS sequence, both the expression level and functional characteristics of the target protein can be enhanced, thereby providing robust support for advancements in synthetic biology and metabolic engineering. Looking ahead, with the continued development of high-throughput screening technologies and computational prediction models, RBS optimization strategies are anticipated to become increasingly precise and efficient, further driving innovation in related fields.
                - generic [ref=e869]:
                  - heading "3.2. DNA assembly techniques" [level=3] [ref=e870]
                  - paragraph [ref=e871]:
                    - text: DNA assembly is a crucial fundamental technology in synthetic biology and the cornerstone of gene expression cassette and pathway engineering (
                    - link "He et al., 2023" [ref=e872] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0305"
                    - text: ;
                    - link "Shao et al., 2009" [ref=e873] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0785"
                    - text: ). Beginning in the 1970 s, this area has undergone substantial transformations, initially through restriction endonuclease-based digestion and ligation techniques, which established the groundwork for precise DNA manipulation. In response to limitations concerning scalability and adaptability, scientists have invested considerable effort into creating more efficient, accurate, modular, and economical DNA assembly technologies (
                    - link "Gibson et al., 2009" [ref=e874] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0255"
                    - text: ). Nevertheless, challenges persist in harmonizing assembly processes across various host organisms. Ongoing refinement of these methods, particularly via automation and standardization, remains essential to satisfy the increasing requirements of synthetic biology applications.
                  - paragraph [ref=e875]:
                    - text: "Depending on the distinct assembly mechanisms, DNA assembly methods can typically be classified into several categories: Those that rely on DNA polymerases, endonucleases (e.g., BioBrick, BglBrick, ePathBrick, MASTER, Golden Gate), exonucleases [e.g., Gibson, Sequence and Ligation Independent Cloning (SLIC)], and enzyme-independent assembly strategies (e.g., enzyme-free cloning, thermal paste annealing) ("
                    - link "Fig. 7B" [ref=e876] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#f0035"
                    - text: ) (
                    - link "Bird et al., 2022" [ref=e877] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0050"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "García-Nafría et al., 2016" [ref=e878] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0240"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Li and Elledge, 2007" [ref=e879] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0480"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Liang et al., 2017" [ref=e880] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0490"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "TerMaat et al., 2009" [ref=e881] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0870"
                    - text: "). A notable study introduced a seamless and highly efficient start-stop assembly method for multi-element integration, enabling the incorporation of up to 60 elements into an expression plasmid with 15 expression units. Each unit comprises four key components: A promoter, an RBS sequence, a CDS, and a terminator. This technique greatly enhances the assembly of extremely long pathways and the optimization of expression unit combinations ("
                    - link "Taylor, Mordaka, & Heap, 2019" [ref=e882] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0865"
                    - text: ). Overall, these approaches offer a practical and effective means for building longer and more intricate genomic sequences.
                  - paragraph [ref=e883]:
                    - text: Currently, DNA assembly methods play a crucial role in synthetic biology by enabling the construction and optimization of intricate metabolic pathways. research designed several modules incorporating genes from opioid biosynthetic pathways, successfully combining 21 diverse genes along with two yeast-originated genes through the Gibson and DNA assembler techniques. These modules were subsequently incorporated into yeast chromosomes or plasmids for expression, leading to engineered strains that could produce opioid compounds (
                    - link "Galanie, Thodey, Trenchard, Filsinger Interrante, & Smolke, 2015" [ref=e884] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0225"
                    - text: ). Additionally, studies applied the Golden Gate approach to develop a library of violacein-producing strains in
                    - emphasis [ref=e885]: Y. lipolytica
                    - text: ", attaining a violacein yield of 70.04 mg/L under optimized growth conditions ("
                    - link "Tong, Zhou, Zhang, & Xu, 2021" [ref=e886] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0895"
                    - text: ). In conclusion, DNA assembly technology offers a powerful and flexible framework for synthesizing bioactive compounds via extensive and complicated metabolic routes.
                - generic [ref=e887]:
                  - heading "3.3. Genome editing techniques" [level=3] [ref=e888]
                  - paragraph [ref=e889]:
                    - text: The development of efficient genome editing tools is crucial for the construction and improvement of microbial cell factories (
                    - link "Lv et al., 2023" [ref=e890] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0565"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Qian et al., 2023" [ref=e891] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0715"
                    - text: ). Therein, the CRISPR/Cas9 system has become a significant area of focus in recent studies (
                    - link "Lim & Lee, 2024" [ref=e892] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0505"
                    - text: ). This innovative technology allows for highly precise modifications to target genes, enabling actions such as replacement, insertion, suppression, or removal of specific genes to achieve desired cellular characteristics (
                    - link "Westbrook et al., 2016" [ref=e893] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0960"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Pacesa et al., 2024" [ref=e894] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0680"
                    - text: ). Consequently, the CRISPR/Cas9 system has been successfully applied across a range of microorganisms, including
                    - emphasis [ref=e895]: E. coli, Bacillus subtilis
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e896]: C. glutamicum
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e897]: S. cerevisiae
                    - text: and so on (
                    - link "Cai et al., 2022" [ref=e898] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0075"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Mózsik et al., 2021" [ref=e899] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0630"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Peng et al., 2017" [ref=e900] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0705"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Sachla et al., 2021" [ref=e901] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0755"
                    - text: ). The core mechanism of the CRISPR/Cas9 system relies on the Cas9 protein (
                    - link "Mir et al., 2018" [ref=e902] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0615"
                    - text: ). Guided by the RNA sequence, the Cas9 protein identifies DNA regions containing the protospacer adjacent motif (PAM) and induces accurate cuts at the designated location, leading to a double-strand break (
                    - link "Fig. 7C" [ref=e903] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#f0035"
                    - text: ). Following the introduction of a break, the cell activates its intrinsic repair pathways, such as non-homologous end joining (NHEJ) or homologous recombination (HR), to restore the damaged site (
                    - link "Lv et al., 2023" [ref=e904] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0565"
                    - text: ). Consequently, a variety of targeted mutations can be generated. Currently, To date, the CRISPR/Cas9 system has been effectively utilized for the biosynthesis of terpenes, flavonoids, and alkaloids in microorganisms (
                    - link "Marsafari and Xu, 2020" [ref=e905] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0595"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Moon et al., 2020" [ref=e906] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0625"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Sun et al., 2024" [ref=e907] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0825"
                    - text: ). For example, a study employed the CRISPR/Cas9 technology to integrate expression units of four key enzymes involved in resveratrol biosynthesis into the genome of
                    - emphasis [ref=e908]: S. cerevisiae
                    - text: . After optimization, the resveratrol production was increased to 151.6 mg/L (
                    - link "Costa et al., 2021" [ref=e909] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0145"
                    - text: ). Moreover, this approach has been extended to modify
                    - emphasis [ref=e910]: E. coli
                    - text: for the development of
                    - emphasis [ref=e911]: L
                    - text: "-tyrosine-producing chassis cells. Further refinements enabled efficient ("
                    - emphasis [ref=e912]: S
                    - text: )-canadine biosynthesis, achieving a final titer of 165.74 mg/L (
                    - link "Zhang, Wu, Huang, Liu, & Wang, 2024" [ref=e913] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b1110"
                    - text: ).
                  - paragraph [ref=e914]:
                    - text: Despite its advantages, the CRISPR/Cas9 system encounters several challenges, such as sequence limitations, off-target effects, and potential genetic toxicity (
                    - link "Pacesa et al., 2022" [ref=e915] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0675"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Sreekanth et al., 2020" [ref=e916] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#b0815"
                    - text: ). To overcome these obstacles, innovative techniques have been introduced to improve the efficiency and specificity of genome editing. For instance, in 2018, research devised a CRISPR-based gRNA-free one-step genome editing method called CAGO for use in
                    - emphasis [ref=e917]: E. coli
                    - text: .
